It’s more like it just comes up and I get stuck on it, rather than me feeling like I have to follow some rule with it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you can't think about your father's decline in the way that you usually do?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I don’t think I get anxious because I can’t think about it in a certain way. It’s more that the thoughts come on their own, and if I’m distracted, they just come back later.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to perform specific actions to try and get rid of these thoughts about being punished?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think I do anything specific to make the thoughts go away. I might try to distract myself, but there isn’t a ritual or certain action I feel like I have to do. The thoughts just kind of keep coming back.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you must behave in a certain way to prevent something bad from happening as a result of your thoughts about being punished?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I don’t feel like I have to act a certain way to stop something from happening. The thoughts are upsetting, but I’m not doing specific things because of them.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. It feels like I deserve whatever happens.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to think about your father's decline in a specific way or for a certain amount of time?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so. I do think about it a lot, but not in a set way or for a certain amount of time. It’s more like it just comes up and I get stuck on it, rather than me feeling like I have to follow some rule with it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you can't think about your father's decline in the way that you usually do?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I don’t think I get anxious because I can’t think about it in a certain way. It’s more that the thoughts come on their own, and if I’m distracted, they just come back later.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to perform specific actions to try and get rid of these thoughts about being punished?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think I do anything specific to make the thoughts go away. I might try to distract myself, but there isn’t a ritual or certain action I feel like I have to do. The thoughts just kind of keep coming back.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you must behave in a certain way to prevent something bad from happening as a result of your thoughts about being punished?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I don’t feel like I have to act a certain way to stop something from happening. The thoughts are upsetting, but I’m not doing specific things because of them.\n\nDoctor: Do you find pleasure in activities that you used to enjoy before your father's decline?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I haven’t been paying much attention to what I enjoy lately. It’s hard for me to say.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find pleasure in or enjoy doing?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I can’t really think of anything specific right now that I still enjoy. Mostly I just kind of get through things without noticing much pleasure in them.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in activities that you used to find enjoyable?\n\nPatient: I don’t know for sure. I haven’t really been paying attention to what I enjoy anymore. It feels like I’m just going through the motions, but I can’t clearly say what I’ve lost interest in.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n14\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF43.8, F32.0, F42.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I came in because I can’t get past this feeling—really this certainty—that I’m being punished for letting my father decline. My sister keeps showing me the doctor’s notes and telling me I did everything I was supposed to do, but it doesn’t change it. It feels like some kind of judgment has already been passed on me.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself constantly thinking about your father's decline and feeling like you're being punished, even when you're trying to focus on other things?\n\nPatient: Yes. It’s there all the time, even when I’m trying to think about work or anything else. I keep coming back to the idea that I let my father decline, and that I’m being punished for it. My sister can show me the notes and say I did what I was supposed to do, but it doesn’t really change how certain it feels to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel a strong urge to repeat certain behaviors or mental acts in response to these thoughts of being punished?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I don’t think I have a specific thing I have to repeat, like a ritual. It’s more that the thought just keeps coming back, and I sit with it or go over it in my head. But I haven’t noticed myself needing to do certain actions over and over because of it.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or down most of the time since your father's decline?\n\nPatient: Yes. Most days I wake up with this heavy, hollow feeling. It’s like the color has gone out of things. I can be sitting at my desk at work, or looking at the garden beds, and none of it really reaches me anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty about your father's decline?\n\nPatient: Yes. I feel guilty all the time. I keep replaying every decision about his appointments, and every tense conversation with my relatives. I keep ending up at the same place—that I was selfish as a daughter, and now I’m useless at work too. It feels like I deserve whatever happens.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to think about your father's decline in a specific way or for a certain amount of time?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so. I do think about it a lot, but not in a set way or for a certain amount of time. It’s more like it just comes up and I get stuck on it, rather than me feeling like I have to follow some rule with it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you can't think about your father's decline in the way that you usually do?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I don’t think I get anxious because I can’t think about it in a certain way. It’s more that the thoughts come on their own, and if I’m distracted, they just come back later.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to perform specific actions to try and get rid of these thoughts about being punished?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think I do anything specific to make the thoughts go away. I might try to distract myself, but there isn’t a ritual or certain action I feel like I have to do. The thoughts just kind of keep coming back.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you must behave in a certain way to prevent something bad from happening as a result of your thoughts about being punished?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I don’t feel like I have to act a certain way to stop something from happening. The thoughts are upsetting, but I’m not doing specific things because of them.\n\nDoctor: Do you find pleasure in activities that you used to enjoy before your father's decline?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I haven’t been paying much attention to what I enjoy lately. It’s hard for me to say.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find pleasure in or enjoy doing?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I can’t really think of anything specific right now that I still enjoy. I haven’t really been paying attention to what I enjoy anymore. It feels like I’m just going through the motions, but I can’t clearly say what I’ve lost interest in.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this heavy, hollow feeling and like the color has gone out of things?\n\nPatient: It’s been about a year and two months, I think. I keep ending up at the same place—that I was selfish as a daughter, and now I’m useless at work too. It feels like I deserve whatever happens.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to think about your father's decline in a specific way or for a certain amount of time?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so. I do think about it a lot, but not in a set way or for a certain amount of time. It’s more like it just comes up and I get stuck on it, rather than me feeling like I have to follow some rule with it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you can't think about your father's decline in the way that you usually do?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I don’t think I get anxious because I can’t think about it in a certain way. It’s more that the thoughts come on their own, and if I’m distracted, they just come back later.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to perform specific actions to try and get rid of these thoughts about being punished?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think I do anything specific to make the thoughts go away. I might try to distract myself, but there isn’t a ritual or certain action I feel like I have to do. The thoughts just kind of keep coming back.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you must behave in a certain way to prevent something bad from happening as a result of your thoughts about being punished?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I don’t feel like I have to act a certain way to stop something from happening. The thoughts are upsetting, but I’m not doing specific things because of them.\n\nDoctor: Do you find pleasure in activities that you used to enjoy before your father's decline?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I haven’t been paying much attention to what I enjoy lately. It’s hard for me to say.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find pleasure in or enjoy doing?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I can’t really think of anything specific right now that I still enjoy. Mostly I just kind of get through things without noticing much pleasure in them.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in activities that you used to find enjoyable?\n\nPatient: I don’t know for sure. I haven’t really been paying attention to what I enjoy anymore. It feels like I’m just going through the motions, but I can’t clearly say what I’ve lost interest in.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this heavy, hollow feeling and like the color has gone out of things?\n\nPatient: It’s been about a year and two months, I think. It started around when my father’s health and behavior began changing, and I had to step in more with taking care of him. Since then it’s just kind of been there.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to think certain thoughts or perform certain mental acts to neutralize or cope with the thoughts of being punished?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I don’t think I have any specific thoughts I have to repeat or anything like that. I mostly just feel scared or weighed down by it, but I haven’t noticed a set thing I do in my head to make it go away.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts about being punished are unwanted and intrusive?\n\nPatient: Yes. I don’t want them there, but it’s more than just a thought popping in. I feel certain I’m being punished for letting my father decline. Even when my sister shows me the doctor’s notes or tells me I did everything I could, it still feels like some judgment has already been passed on me.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

I can be sitting at my desk at work, or looking at the garden beds, and none of it really reaches me anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty about your father's decline?\n\nPatient: Yes. I feel guilty all the time. I keep replaying every decision about his appointments, and every tense conversation with my relatives. I keep ending up at the same place—that I was selfish as a daughter, and now I’m useless at work too. It feels like I deserve whatever happens.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to think about your father's decline in a specific way or for a certain amount of time?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so. I do think about it a lot, but not in a set way or for a certain amount of time. It’s more like it just comes up and I get stuck on it, rather than me feeling like I have to follow some rule with it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you can't think about your father's decline in the way that you usually do?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I don’t think I get anxious because I can’t think about it in a certain way. It’s more that the thoughts come on their own, and if I’m distracted, they just come back later.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to perform specific actions to try and get rid of these thoughts about being punished?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think I do anything specific to make the thoughts go away. I might try to distract myself, but there isn’t a ritual or certain action I feel like I have to do. The thoughts just kind of keep coming back.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you must behave in a certain way to prevent something bad from happening as a result of your thoughts about being punished?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I don’t feel like I have to act a certain way to stop something from happening. The thoughts are upsetting, but I’m not doing specific things because of them.\n\nDoctor: Do you find pleasure in activities that you used to enjoy before your father's decline?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I haven’t been paying much attention to what I enjoy lately. It’s hard for me to say.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find pleasure in or enjoy doing?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I can’t really think of anything specific right now that I still enjoy. Mostly I just kind of get through things without noticing much pleasure in them.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in activities that you used to find enjoyable?\n\nPatient: I don’t know for sure. I haven’t really been paying attention to what I enjoy anymore. It feels like I’m just going through the motions, but I can’t clearly say what I’ve lost interest in.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this heavy, hollow feeling and like the color has gone out of things?\n\nPatient: It’s been about a year and two months, I think. It started around when my father’s health and behavior began changing, and I had to step in more with taking care of him. Since then it’s just kind of been there.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to think certain thoughts or perform certain mental acts to neutralize or cope with the thoughts of being punished?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I don’t think I have any specific thoughts I have to repeat or anything like that. I mostly just feel scared or weighed down by it, but I haven’t noticed a set thing I do in my head to make it go away.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts about being punished are unwanted and intrusive?\n\nPatient: Yes. I don’t want them there, but it’s more than just a thought popping in. I feel certain I’m being punished for letting my father decline. Even when my sister shows me the doctor’s notes or tells me I did everything I could, it still feels like some judgment has already been passed on me.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts about being punished are interfering with your daily life?\n\nPatient: Yes. It’s affecting a lot. At work I’ve missed deadlines, and I’ve been avoiding the harder employee meetings that I normally would handle. And I stopped going to my library volunteer shifts because I just can’t keep myself steady enough to follow through.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n18\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF43.8, F32.0\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I came in because I can’t get past this feeling—really this certainty—that I’m being punished for letting my father decline. My sister keeps showing me the doctor’s notes and telling me I did everything I was supposed to do, but it doesn’t change it. It feels like some kind of judgment has already been passed on me.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself constantly thinking about your father's decline and feeling like you're being punished, even when you're trying to focus on other things?\n\nPatient: Yes. It’s there all the time, even when I’m trying to think about work or anything else. I keep coming back to the idea that I let my father decline, and that I’m being punished for it. My sister can show me the notes and say I did what I was supposed to do, but it doesn’t really change how certain it feels to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel a strong urge to repeat certain behaviors or mental acts in response to these thoughts of being punished?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I don’t think I have a specific thing I have to repeat, like a ritual. It’s more that the thought just keeps coming back, and I sit with it or go over it in my head. But I haven’t noticed myself needing to do certain actions over and over because of it.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or down most of the time since your father's decline?\n\nPatient: Yes. Most days I wake up with this heavy, hollow feeling. It’s like the color has gone out of things. I can be sitting at my desk at work, or looking at the garden beds, and none of it really reaches me anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty about your father's decline?\n\nPatient: Yes. I feel guilty all the time. I keep replaying every decision about his appointments, and every tense conversation with my relatives. I keep ending up at the same place—that I was selfish as a daughter, and now I’m useless at work too. It feels like I deserve whatever happens.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to think about your father's decline in a specific way or for a certain amount of time?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so. I do think about it a lot, but not in a set way or for a certain amount of time. It’s more like it just comes up and I get stuck on it, rather than me feeling like I have to follow some rule with it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you can't think about your father's decline in the way that you usually do?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I don’t think I get anxious because I can’t think about it in a certain way. It’s more that the thoughts come on their own, and if I’m distracted, they just come back later.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to perform specific actions to try and get rid of these thoughts about being punished?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think I do anything specific to make the thoughts go away. I might try to distract myself, but there isn’t a ritual or certain action I feel like I have to do. The thoughts just kind of keep coming back.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you must behave in a certain way to prevent something bad from happening as a result of your thoughts about being punished?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I don’t feel like I have to act a certain way to stop something from happening. The thoughts are upsetting, but I’m not doing specific things because of them.\n\nDoctor: Do you find pleasure in activities that you used to enjoy before your father's decline?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I haven’t been paying much attention to what I enjoy lately. It’s hard for me to say.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find pleasure in or enjoy doing?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I can’t really think of anything specific right now that I still enjoy. Mostly I just kind of get through things without noticing much pleasure in them.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in activities that you used to find enjoyable?\n\nPatient: I don’t know for sure. I haven’t really been paying attention to what I enjoy anymore. It feels like I’m just going through the motions, but I can’t clearly say what I’ve lost interest in.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this heavy, hollow feeling and like the color has gone out of things?\n\nPatient: It’s been about a year and two months, I think. It started around when my father’s health and behavior began changing, and I had to step in more with taking care of him. Since then it’s just kind of been there.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to think certain thoughts or perform certain mental acts to neutralize or cope with the thoughts of being punished?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I don’t think I have any specific thoughts I have to repeat or anything like that. I mostly just feel scared or weighed down by it, but I haven’t noticed a set thing I do in my head to make it go away.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts about being punished are unwanted and intrusive?\n\nPatient: Yes. I don’t want them there, but it’s more than just a thought popping in. I feel certain I’m being punished for letting my father decline. Even when my sister shows me the doctor’s notes or tells me I did everything I could, it still feels like some judgment has already been passed on me.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts about being punished are interfering with your daily life?\n\nPatient: Yes. It’s affecting a lot. At work I’ve missed deadlines, and I’ve been avoiding the harder employee meetings that I normally would handle. And I stopped going to my library volunteer shifts because I just can’t keep myself steady enough to follow through.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts about being punished are repeating in your mind against your will?\n\nPatient: Yes, it does feel like it keeps coming back even when I don’t want it to. I’ll try to tell myself what my sister said, that the doctors’ notes show I did things right, but it doesn’t really change it. I still feel certain that I’m being punished for letting my father decline, like some judgment has already been made about me.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight since your father's decline?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I haven’t been keeping track closely, and I haven’t noticed a big change one way or the other.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

It feels like some kind of judgment has already been passed on me.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself constantly thinking about your father's decline and feeling like you're being punished, even when you're trying to focus on other things?\n\nPatient: Yes. It’s there all the time, even when I’m trying to think about work or anything else. I keep coming back to the idea that I let my father decline, and that I’m being punished for it. My sister can show me the notes and say I did what I was supposed to do, but it doesn’t really change how certain it feels to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel a strong urge to repeat certain behaviors or mental acts in response to these thoughts of being punished?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I don’t think I have a specific thing I have to repeat, like a ritual. It’s more that the thought just keeps coming back, and I sit with it or go over it in my head. But I haven’t noticed myself needing to do certain actions over and over because of it.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or down most of the time since your father's decline?\n\nPatient: Yes. Most days I wake up with this heavy, hollow feeling. It’s like the color has gone out of things. I can be sitting at my desk at work, or looking at the garden beds, and none of it really reaches me anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty about your father's decline?\n\nPatient: Yes. I feel guilty all the time. I keep replaying every decision about his appointments, and every tense conversation with my relatives. I keep ending up at the same place—that I was selfish as a daughter, and now I’m useless at work too. It feels like I deserve whatever happens.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to think about your father's decline in a specific way or for a certain amount of time?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so. I do think about it a lot, but not in a set way or for a certain amount of time. It’s more like it just comes up and I get stuck on it, rather than me feeling like I have to follow some rule with it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you can't think about your father's decline in the way that you usually do?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I don’t think I get anxious because I can’t think about it in a certain way. It’s more that the thoughts come on their own, and if I’m distracted, they just come back later.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to perform specific actions to try and get rid of these thoughts about being punished?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think I do anything specific to make the thoughts go away. I might try to distract myself, but there isn’t a ritual or certain action I feel like I have to do. I don’t feel like I have to act a certain way to stop something from happening. The thoughts are upsetting, but I’m not doing specific things because of them.\n\nDoctor: Do you find pleasure in activities that you used to enjoy before your father's decline?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I haven’t been paying much attention to what I enjoy lately. It’s hard for me to say.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find pleasure in or enjoy doing?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I can’t really think of anything specific right now that I still enjoy. I haven’t really been paying attention to what I enjoy anymore. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"

Most days I wake up with this heavy, hollow feeling. It’s like the color has gone out of things. I don’t think I get anxious because I can’t think about it in a certain way.
